    "Title": "Data Augmentation for Meta-Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper systematically dissects the meta-learning pipeline, identifying four distinct modes of data augmentation (support, query, task, and shot) and empirically showing that meta-learners are highly sensitive to query data and task diversity. It proposes Meta-MaxUp, a novel data augmentation strategy that extends MaxUp to meta-learning by maximizing loss over a pool of augmentations to improve generalization and prevent overfitting. This approach significantly boosts the performance of popular meta-learning algorithms on few-shot classification benchmarks (mini-ImageNet, CIFAR-FS) and demonstrates strong cross-domain generalization on Meta-Dataset, outperforming existing methods and achieving state-of-the-art results in some inductive few-shot settings.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology involves analyzing the sensitivity of meta-learning algorithms to support, query, and task data diversity. Four data augmentation modes are introduced: support augmentation (applied to inner-loop support data), query augmentation (applied to evaluation data), task augmentation (increasing task diversity by uniformly augmenting whole classes), and shot augmentation (amplifying test-time shots and training with similar support augmentations). A variety of standard (rotation, horizontal flip, random erase) and advanced (CutMix, MixUp, Self-Mix) augmentation techniques are applied within these modes. The core technical contribution is Meta-MaxUp, which adapts the MaxUp strategy to meta-learning. This involves sampling multiple augmentations for each task during training, selecting the augmented task that maximizes the outer-loop loss, and then performing a parameter update to minimize this maximized loss. This process is formalized as a minimax optimization problem: min_θ E_T [ max_M∈S L(F_θ', M(Tq)) ], where θ' is obtained by fine-tuning A(θ, M(Ts)).",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on four meta-learning algorithms: ProtoNet, R2-D2, MetaOptNet, and MCT, using ResNet-12 and 4-layer convolutional network (CNN-4) backbones. The primary datasets used are mini-ImageNet and CIFAR-FS, which are few-shot learning datasets derived from ImageNet and CIFAR-100, respectively, each with 64 training, 16 validation, and 20 testing classes. Cross-domain generalization was evaluated on Meta-Dataset, a benchmark consisting of 10 diverse image classification subdatasets, with models trained on ILSVRC-2012 and tested on the other 9. Evaluation metrics include 1-shot and 5-shot classification accuracy with confidence intervals (one standard error). Ablation studies were performed to assess the impact of data diversity on support, query, and tasks. Meta-MaxUp's performance was evaluated by varying the number of sampled augmentations (m) and the size of the augmentation pool (Single, Medium, Large). Training involved 60 epochs, 8000 episodes per epoch, mini-batches of 8 tasks, and was performed on 4 Nvidia RTX 2080 Ti GPUs. All comparisons were made between inductive learning methods.",
    "Limitations": "The study found that support augmentation often degrades performance. Combining different augmentations requires careful selection, as not all combinations lead to improvements; for instance, applying CutMix to both support and query data can result in worse performance than applying it solely to query data. The proposed strategies are not universally effective across all datasets, specifically, Omniglot (comprising handwritten letters) shows decreased performance because its characteristics are not invariant to the strong augmentations used, indicating a need for specially designed augmentations for such data. Additionally, the Meta-MaxUp strategy, while effective, increases training time by approximately 2-3 times compared to baseline methods due to the requirement for multiple forward passes to select the loss-maximizing augmentation.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that this work opens up possibilities for further research into meta-specific data augmentation strategies. They hope that emerging methods for data augmentation will continue to boost the performance of meta-learning algorithms, particularly when applied to progressively larger models with more complex backbones. A specific direction highlighted is the development of specially designed augmentations tailored for datasets like Omniglot to optimize performance where current strong augmentations may be detrimental.",
    "Experiment Code": "def mixup_criterion(opt, pred, y_a, y_b, lam):\n    n_q = opt.train_way * opt.train_query\n    b = opt.episodes_per_batch\n    m = opt.m\n    pred = pred.reshape(-1,opt.train_way)\n    logit = F.log_softmax(pred, dim=-1)\n    loss_a = F.nll_loss(logit, y_a.reshape(-1),reduction='none')\n    loss_b = F.nll_loss(logit, y_b.reshape(-1),reduction='none')\n    loss = loss_a.view(b,m,-1) * lam.view(b,m,1).cuda() + loss_b.view(b,m,-1) * (1 - lam).view(b,m,1).cuda()\n    loss, loc = loss.max(dim=1)\n\n    return loss.mean(), loc\n\ndef rand_bbox(size, lam=0.5):\n    W = size[-1]\n    H = size[-2]\n    cut_rat = np.sqrt(1. - lam)\n    cut_w = np.int(W * cut_rat)\n    cut_h = np.int(H * cut_rat)\n\n    ## uniform\n    cx = np.random.randint(W)\n    cy = np.random.randint(H)\n\n    bbx1 = np.clip(cx - cut_w // 2, 0, W)\n    bby1 = np.clip(cy - cut_h // 2, 0, H)\n    bbx2 = np.clip(cx + cut_w // 2, 0, W)\n    bby2 = np.clip(cy + cut_h // 2, 0, H)\n\n    return bbx1, bby1, bbx2, bby2\n\ndef self_mix(data):\n    size = data.size()\n    W = size[-1]\n    H = size[-2]\n    ## uniform\n    cx = np.random.randint(W)\n    cy = np.random.randint(H)\n\n    cut_w = W//2\n    cut_h = H//2\n\n    bbx1 = np.clip(cx - cut_w // 2, 0, W)\n    bby1 = np.clip(cy - cut_h // 2, 0, H)\n    bbx2 = np.clip(cx + cut_w // 2, 0, W)\n    bby2 = np.clip(cy + cut_h // 2, 0, H)\n\n    while True:\n        bbxn = np.random.randint(0, W-(bbx2-bbx1))\n        bbyn = np.random.randint(0, H-(bby2-bby1))\n\n        if bbxn != bbx1 or bbyn != bby1:\n            break\n\n    ## random rotate the croped when it's square\n    if (bbx2 - bbx1) == (bby2 - bby1):\n        k = random.sample([0, 1, 2, 3], 1)[0]\n    else:\n        k = 0\n    data[:, :, bbx1:bbx2, bby1:bby2] = torch.rot90(data[:, :, bbxn:bbxn + (bbx2-bbx1), bbyn:bbyn + (bby2-bby1)], k, [2,3])\n\n    return data\n\ndef random_rotation(data, r, opt):\n\n    num = data.shape[0] * data.shape[1]\n    ppp = np.random.rand(num) < r\n    degrees = torch.randint(-opt.rot_degree, opt.rot_degree, (num,)) * ppp\n    out = KF.apply_rotation(\n        data.view([-1] + list(data.shape[-3:])), {'degrees': torch.tensor(degrees), 'interpolation': torch.tensor([1]), 'align_corners': torch.tensor(True)})\n\n    return out.view(data.shape)\n\ndef random_erase(data, opt):\n    rec_er = K.RandomErasing(0.5, (.02, .4), (.3, 1/.3))\n    out = rec_er(data.view([-1] + list(data.shape[-3:])))\n\n    return out.view(data.shape)\n\ndef large_rotation(data, opt):\n\n    degrees = torch.randint(4,(data.shape[0] * data.shape[1],)) * 90\n\n    out = KF.apply_rotation(\n        data.view([-1] + list(data.shape[-3:])), {'degrees': torch.tensor(degrees), 'interpolation': opt)\n\n    new_data_s.transpose_(0,1)\n    new_data_q.transpose_(0,1)\n    label_s.transpose_(0,1)\n    label_a.transpose_(0,1)\n    label_b.transpose_(0,1)\n    ls.transpose_(0,1)\n\n    return new_data_s, label_s, new_data_q, label_a, label_b, ls.reshape(-1)\n\ndef shot_aug(data_support, labels_support, n_support, method, opt):\n    size = data_support.shape\n    if method == \"fliplr\":\n        n_support = opt.s_du * n_support\n        data_shot = flip(data_support, -1)\n        data_support = torch.cat((data_support, data_shot), dim = 1)\n        labels_support = torch.cat((labels_support, labels_support), dim = 1)\n    elif method == \"flip_ver\":\n        n_support = opt.s_du * n_support\n        data_shot = flip(data_support, -2)\n        data_support = torch.cat((data_support, data_shot), dim = 1)\n        labels_support = torch.cat((labels_support, labels_support), dim = 1)\n    elif method == \"random_crop\":\n        n_support = opt.s_du * n_support\n        data_shot = random_cropping(data_support, 32)\n        data_shot2 = random_cropping(data_support, 32)\n        data_support = torch.cat((data_shot.view([size[0], -1] + list(data_support.shape[-3:])), data_shot2.view([size[0], -1] + list(data_support.shape[-3:]))), dim = 1)\n        labels_support = torch.cat((labels_support, labels_support), dim = 1)\n    elif method == \"random_rotation\":\n        n_support = opt.s_du * n_support\n        data_shot = random_rotation(data_support, 1, opt)\n        data_support = torch.cat((data_support, data_shot), dim = 1)\n        labels_support = torch.cat((labels_support, labels_support), dim = 1)\n    elif method == \"random_erase\":\n        n_support = opt.s_du * n_support\n        data_shot = random_erase(data_support, opt)\n        data_support = torch.cat((data_support, data_shot), dim = 1)\n        labels_support = torch.cat((labels_support, labels_support), dim = 1)\n    elif method == \"large_rotation\":\n        n_support = opt.s_du * n_support\n        data_shot = large_rotation(data_support, labels_support, opt)\n        data_support = torch.cat((data_support, data_shot), dim = 1)\n        labels_support = torch.cat((labels_support, labels_support), dim = 1)\n    elif method == \"random_solarize\":\n        n_support = opt.s_du * n_support\n        data_shot = labels_support), dim = 1)\n\n    return data_support, labels_support, n_support\n\nda_pool = ['qcm', 'qre', 'sre', 'tlr', 'qcm + tlr', 'qre + tlr']",
    "Experiment Result": "Training Configuration:\n- Number of epochs: 60\n- Model saving frequency: Every 10 epochs\n- Optimizer: SGD\n  - Learning rate: Initial 0.1, with a LambdaLR scheduler (1.0 for epochs < 20, 0.06 for < 40, 0.012 for < 50, 0.0024 for < 60, 0.001 afterwards)\n  - Momentum: 0.9\n  - Weight decay: 5e-4\n  - Nesterov: True\n- Loss Function: CrossEntropyLoss\n\nMeta-learning Task Settings:\n- Training N-way: 5\n- Validation/Test N-way: 5\n- Training K-shot: 5\n- Validation K-shot: 5\n- Training Query examples per class: 6\n- Validation Query examples per class: 15\n- Episodes per batch: 8\n- Training Epoch Size (episodes_per_batch * num_per_batch): 8 * 1000 = 8000 episodes\n- Validation Episodes: 2000\n\nMeta-MaxUp Specific Parameters:\n- `m`: 4 (Number of augmentations sampled for each task to find the one maximizing outer-loop loss)\n- Augmentation Pool (`da_pool` used for maximization): ['qcm', 'qre', 'sre', 'tlr', 'qcm + tlr', 'qre + tlr']\n\nData Augmentation Settings:\n- Feature-level Augmentation (`feat_aug`): 'norm' (Includes RandomCrop (padding 4 for CIFAR-FS, 8 for MiniImageNet), ColorJitter (brightness=0.4, contrast=0.4, saturation=0.4), RandomHorizontalFlip, ToTensor, Normalize)\n- Task-level Augmentation (`task_aug`): Implemented through `da_pool` within `data_aug_maxup` function. Each task is augmented with one method randomly sampled from `da_pool`.\n- Support-level Augmentation (`support_aug`): None by default. If 'mix' is specified, it uses `data_aug_mix` with `mixup` or `cutmix`.\n- Shot-level Augmentation (`shot_aug`): Empty list by default. Can include 'fliplr', 'flip_ver', 'random_crop', 'random_rotation', 'random_erase', 'large_rotation', 'random_solarize', 'shearX', 'self_aug'. `s_du` (support duplication factor) is 1 by default.\n- Query-level Augmentation (`query_aug`): None by default. Similar to support augmentation but applied to query set.\n- Probability for task/support/query augmentations (`t_p`, `s_p`, `q_p`): 1.0 by default for `train_maxup.py`.\n- Rotation Degree (`rot_degree`): 30 degrees for random rotation.\n\nModel and Dataset:\n- Embedding Network (`network`): ProtoNet (default). Alternatives include R2D2, ResNet (with specific DropBlock sizes for different datasets), R2D2_mixup, ResNet_mixup.\n- Classification Head (`head`): ProtoNet (default). Alternatives include Ridge, R2D2, SVM (MetaOptNetHead_SVM_CS).\n- Dataset (`dataset`): miniImageNet (default). Alternatives include CIFAR_FS, FC100."
}{
    "Title": "Frozen Feature Augmentation for Few-Shot Image Classification",
    "Main Contributions": "This paper conducts an extensive pilot study exploring data augmentations in the frozen feature space, termed 'frozen feature augmentation (FroFA)', for few-shot image classification. The main contributions include demonstrating that simple point-wise FroFAs, such as brightness, consistently improve few-shot performance across various network architectures, pretraining datasets, and transfer datasets. The study introduces per-channel stochasticity in augmentations (cFroFA and c2FroFA variants), which further enhances performance. Specifically, FroFA yields significant absolute mean accuracy gains of 3.2% to 4.2% on smaller transfer datasets in the 5-shot setting, and modest but consistent improvements on ImageNet, outperforming linear probe baselines in most few-shot scenarios.",
    "Methodology": "The method involves extracting frozen features from pretrained vision transformers (e.g., ViT outputs) and then applying augmentations in this feature space before training a lightweight model on top. Features, typically 2D (N patches x C channels), are first reshaped to a 3D representation (sqrt(N) x sqrt(N) x C) to align with image augmentation structures. A deterministic feature-to-image mapping scales feature values to a standard image range (e.g., [0,1]), on which standard image augmentations are applied. The augmented 'image-like' features are then mapped back to their original feature value range. Three FroFA variations are explored: Default FroFA (single random value across the entire feature), Channel FroFA (independent random values per channel), and Channel2 FroFA (independent random values per channel, with per-channel min/max scaling). A lightweight multi-head attention pooling (MAP) layer followed by a classification layer is trained on these augmented frozen features. Baselines include a MAP head without augmentation and an L2-regularized linear probe.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The study utilized Ti/16, B/16, and L/16 pretrained Vision Transformer architectures. Pretraining datasets included JFT-3B, ImageNet-21k, and WebLI (with SigLIP). For few-shot transfer, eight downstream datasets were used: ILSVRC-2012 (main benchmark), CIFAR10, CIFAR100, DMLab, DTD, Resisc45, SUN397, and SVHN. Few-shot versions (1-, 5-, 10-, and 25-shot) were created by sampling from the training sets. Eighteen distinct augmentations, categorized into geometric, crop & drop, stylistic, and other (e.g., brightness, contrast, posterize, patch dropout, mixup), were investigated. A base hyperparameter sweep (batch sizes, learning rates, training steps) was conducted for each shot, with best settings chosen via validation sets. Top-1 accuracy was the primary evaluation metric.",
    "Limitations": "Geometric augmentations consistently led to worse performance in the frozen feature space. Initial experiments with element-wise FroFA, which applies augmentations at a finer granularity, resulted in significantly worse performance, hypothesized to be due to substantial changes in feature appearance. Advanced FroFA protocols, such as variations of RandAugment and TrivialAugment using combinations of the best performing FroFAs, showed limited success and did not consistently improve upon the best single augmentation. The study primarily focused on fast training with lightweight models, deferring detailed analysis on larger models to future work.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include conducting a detailed analysis of FroFA on larger models beyond the lightweight head used in this study. A deeper investigation into advanced FroFA protocols, such as more complex combinations or new strategies for RandAugment and TrivialAugment variations, is suggested, as the initial exploration yielded limited success. Further exploration into element-wise FroFA is also a potential avenue to understand why it led to degraded performance and whether it can be made effective.",
    "Experiment Code": "class MlpBlock(nn.Module):\"\"\"Transformer MLP / feed-forward block.\"\"\"mlp_dim: Optional[int] = None  # Defaults to 4x input dimdropout: float = 0.0dtype_mm: str = \"float32\"@nn.compactdef __call__(self, x, deterministic=True):\"\"\"Applies Transformer MlpBlock module.\"\"\"inits = dict(kernel_init=nn.initializers.xavier_uniform(),bias_init=nn.initializers.normal(stddev=1e-6),)d = x.shape[-1]x = nn.Dense(self.mlp_dim or 4 * d, dtype=self.dtype_mm, **inits)(x)# In some extreme batch-size cases, this is needed as of Sept 2024:x = nn.with_logical_constraint(x, (\"act_batch\", \"act_len\", \"act_emb\"))x = nn.gelu(x)x = nn.Dropout(rate=self.dropout)(x, deterministic)x = nn.Dense(d, dtype=self.dtype_mm, **inits)(x)return xclass MAPHead(nn.Module):\"\"\"Multihead Attention Pooling.\"\"\"mlp_dim: Optional[int] = None  # Defaults to 4x input dimnum_heads: int = 12@nn.compactdef __call__(self, x):# TODOn, l, d = x.shape  # pylint: disable=unused-variableprobe = self.param(\"probe\", nn.initializers.xavier_uniform(),(1, 1, d), x.dtype)probe = jnp.tile(probe, [n, 1, 1])x = nn.MultiHeadDotProductAttention(num_heads=self.num_heads,kernel_init=nn.initializers.xavier_uniform())(probe, x)# TODO: dropout on head?y = nn.LayerNorm()(x)x = x + MlpBlock(mlp_dim=self.mlp_dim)(y)return x[:, 0]BIAS_CONSTANT = 100.0# Temporary global flag to facilitate backwards compatability. Will be removed# by the end of year 2023.API = \"jit\"# Setup function for few-shot regression on CPU to avoid \"polluting\" the TPU.@u.jit_cpu(static_argnums=(2,))def _precompute_cache(x, y, num_classes):\"\"\"Cache quantities to speed-up the computation of L2-regularized least-sq.\"\"\"# Whitenmean = jnp.mean(x, axis=0, keepdims=True)std = jnp.std(x, axis=0, keepdims=True) + 1e-5x = (x - mean) / std# Add a constant feature for the bias, large so it's almost unregularized:x = jnp.pad(x, ((0, 0), (0, 1)), constant_values=BIAS_CONSTANT)# To one-hot representation rescaled into {-1, 1}y = 2.0 * jax.nn.one_hot(y, num_classes) - 1.0num_points, dim = x.shape# Let N be the number of points, D the dimension and C the number of classes.# We have x of shape (N, D) and y of shape (N, C).# For least-squares, we can compute##   (A) when N >= D, (x^T x + l2 Id)^{-1} x^T y#   (B) when D > N, x^T  (x x^T + l2 Id)^{-1} y## We pre-compute the eigen-decomposition of either x^T x or x x^T which# becomes q diag(eigs) q^T with q unitary matrix either (D, D) or (N, N)# and eigs a vector (D,) or (N,).## For any l2 > 0, we can compute (x^T x + l2 Id)^{-1} or (x x^T + l2 Id)^{-1}# by simply computing q (diag(eigs) + l2 Id)^{-1} q^T.# (SVD would be more natural here, but it proved slower, so we use eigh)## Both cases (A) and (B) can be viewed as lhs (diag(eigs) + l2 Id)^{-1} rhs,# where lhs/rhs are pre-computed left/right-hand sides to specify.## Detailed evaluation in terms of time and fewshot metrics can be found in# (internal link)## Implemented by Rodolphe Jenatton.if num_points >= dim:eigs, q = jnp.linalg.eigh(x.T @ x)rhs = q.T @ (x.T @ y)lhs = qelse:eigs, q = jnp.linalg.eigh(x @ x.T)rhs = q.T @ ylhs = x.T @ qcache = {\"eigs\": eigs,\"rhs\": rhs,\"lhs\": lhs,\"mean\": mean,\"std\": std}return cache@u.jit_cpu()def _eig_fewshot_acc_fn(cache, x_test, y_test, l2_reg):\"\"\"Computes (x,y) linear regression accuracy on (x_test, y_test).\"\"\"x_test = (x_test - cache[\"mean\"]) / cache[\"std\"]x_test = jnp.pad(x_test, ((0, 0), (0, 1)), constant_values=BIAS_CONSTANT)rhs = cache[\"rhs\"]lhs = cache[\"lhs\"]eigs = cache[\"eigs\"]# See comments in _precompute_cache for context about the formula.scaling = 1.0 / (eigs + l2_reg * jnp.ones_like(eigs))scaling = scaling.reshape((1, -1))w = (lhs * scaling) @ rhs# Predict test-set values and measure their accuracypreds = jnp.argmax(x_test @ w, axis=1)return jnp.mean(preds == y_test)from big_vision.pp import registryfrom big_vision.pp import utilsfrom big_vision.pp.archive import autoaugment@registry.Registry.register(\"preprocess_ops.randaug\")@utils.InKeyOutKey()def get_randaug(num_layers: int = 2, magnitude: int = 10):\"\"\"Creates a function that applies RandAugment.RandAugment is from the paper https://arxiv.org/abs/1909.13719,Args:num_layers: Integer, the number of augmentation transformations to applysequentially to an image. Represented as (N) in the paper. Usually bestvalues will be in the range [1, 3].magnitude: Integer, shared magnitude across all augmentation operations.Represented as (M) in the paper. Usually best values are in the range [5,30].Returns:a function that applies RandAugment.\"\"\"def _randaug(image):return autoaugment.distort_image_with_randaugment(image, num_layers, magnitude)return _randaug",
    "Experiment Result": "The method \"FroFA\" (Frozen Feature Augmentation) trains a lightweight model on frozen features from a pretrained Vision Transformer (ViT).Experimental settings and components related to FroFA:\n- Feature Extraction: Features are extracted from the 'pre_logits' layer of a pretrained ViT model (e.g., 'howto-i21k-B/32'). The original classification 'head' of the ViT is typically frozen by setting 'dont_load=['head/kernel', 'head/bias']' during initialization.\n- Lightweight Model: A Multi-Head Attention Pooling (MAP) layer (specifically, 'pool_type='map'') followed by a classification layer is trained on these frozen features.\n- Baselines/Evaluation:\n    - L2-Regularized Linear Probe: A linear probe with L2 regularization (e.g., 'l2_reg=2.0 ** 10') is used as a baseline for few-shot learning, implemented with a cache for efficient least-squares computation.\n    - Few-shot Settings: Few-shot evaluations are conducted with various shot counts (e.g., '1, 5, 10, 25') and multiple seeds (e.g., 'num_seeds=3').\n- Feature Augmentation (implied): Standard image augmentations (like 'RandAugment') are conceptually applied in the feature space, after reshaping and mapping features to an image-like representation. However, the explicit code for this feature-space reshaping and mapping is not provided in the repository content."
}{
    "Title": "Effective Data Augmentation With Diffusion Models",
    "Main Contributions": "This paper addresses the lack of diversity in standard data augmentation techniques by proposing DA-Fusion, a flexible strategy that uses pre-trained text-to-image diffusion models (specifically Stable Diffusion) to semantically edit images. The method generalizes to novel visual concepts from a few labeled examples. It significantly improves accuracy in few-shot image classification tasks (up to +10 percentage points) across various domains, including common, fine-grain, and previously unseen concepts, and on a real-world weed recognition task. The research also explores methods to prevent leakage of internet training data from large generative models into downstream evaluations.",
    "Methodology": "DA-Fusion adapts a pre-trained Stable Diffusion model by fine-tuning new embeddings (pseudo-prompts) in the text encoder for each concept using Textual Inversion. Instead of generating images from scratch, it uses real images as a guide by splicing them into the reverse diffusion process at a specific timestep, following SDEdit. The generation is guided by the learned pseudo-prompt. To control the augmentation, the method balances real and synthetic data using sampling probabilities (e.g., alpha=0.5) and enhances diversity by randomly sampling the insertion timestep (t0) where real images are introduced into the diffusion process (e.g., k=4 intensities). Leakage prevention is implemented through two strategies: a model-centric approach that fine-tunes the UNet backbone to erase class knowledge (using ESD), and a data-centric approach that removes class names from model inputs.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on few-shot image classification tasks using six standard computer vision datasets: Caltech101, Flowers102, FGVC Aircraft, Stanford Cars, COCO, and PASCAL VOC. A new 'Leafy Spurge' dataset of top-down drone images for weed recognition was also contributed and used for evaluation, representing concepts unseen by Stable Diffusion. A ResNet50 classifier pre-trained on ImageNet was fine-tuned on a mixture of real and synthetic images. Evaluation involved varying the number of examples per class and reporting validation accuracy. Baselines included RandAugment and Real Guidance (SDEdit with descriptive prompts), and ablations were performed for data balance and randomized intensity. Additional experiments included stronger baselines like CutMix and different classifier architectures (DeiT). Leakage prevention mechanisms (model-centric and data-centric) were thoroughly tested on COCO, PASCAL VOC, and Leafy Spurge.",
    "Limitations": "The current method cannot defend against all forms of harmful content, specifically biased content that encourages racial or gender stereotypes, as detecting these is harder than detecting nudity or violence. While a safety checker and editing real images (rather than generating from scratch) are used to mitigate harmful content, they are not perfect solutions. Another limitation, inherent to large-scale generative models like Stable Diffusion, is the potential to generate augmentations that resemble or copy data from users online, due to training on internet data without explicit consent. The model-centric leakage prevention method is computationally costly (two hours per class on a 32GB V100 GPU).",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could focus on explicitly controlling how images are augmented by the diffusion model, for example, by enabling localized edits (e.g., changing the breed of a cat) without pixel-level supervision, potentially using prompt-based image editing techniques. Another direction is maintaining temporal consistency when applying DA-Fusion in decision-making settings, such as visual reinforcement learning, to improve few-shot generalization of policies in complex visual environments. Finally, any advancements in the underlying diffusion model backbone that enhance image photo-realism are expected to directly improve DA-Fusion's performance.",

    "Title": "A Prototype-Oriented Framework for Unsupervised Domain Adaptation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the limitations of existing unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) methods, such as sampling variability, class imbalance, and data-privacy concerns, which typically rely on minimizing statistical distances between source and target samples. It proposes a novel Prototype-Oriented Conditional Transport (PCT) framework. Key contributions include leveraging linear classifier weights as learnable class prototypes for robust feature alignment, introducing a probabilistic bi-directional transport framework to align target features with these prototypes, and a method for estimating target class proportions. The framework is demonstrated to be effective across various UDA scenarios, including single-source, multi-source, class-imbalance, and source-private adaptation, achieving competitive performance without additional model parameters and with a moderate increase in computation.",
    "Methodology": "The Prototype-Oriented Conditional Transport (PCT) is a holistic framework comprising three components. First, class prototypes are learned by interpreting the weights of the linear classifier as learnable prototype parameters in the latent space, using a cross-entropy loss, which introduces no additional model parameters. Second, a probabilistic bi-directional transport framework is used to align target features with these prototypes. This involves minimizing the expected cost of moving from target features to prototypes (Lt→µ), which encourages target features to be near class prototypes, and from prototypes to target features (Lµ→t), which prevents degenerate solutions where target data maps to only a few prototypes. Cosine dissimilarity is used as the point-to-point moving cost. Prototypes are treated as fixed during transport loss calculation for stability. Third, target class proportions are estimated iteratively using an EM algorithm, initialized with a uniform prior, to account for potential shifts in label distributions.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on various image classification datasets: Ofﬁce-31, Ofﬁce-Home, DomainNet, Cross-Digits, ImageClef, Ofﬁce-Caltech, and VisDA. A pre-trained ResNet-50 was primarily used as the feature encoder (ResNet-101 for multi-source DomainNet), with a linear classification layer. Training involved fine-tuning the feature encoder and training linear layers from random initialization using mini-batch SGD with momentum 0.9. A decaying learning rate schedule was applied, with an initial learning rate of 0.01 for linear layers. Batch sizes were N=32 for source and M=96 for target. Experiments were run for 10,000 to 75,000 iterations. Evaluation was based on average accuracy over three independent runs across single-source, multi-source, class-imbalance (sub-sampled source and target data), and source-private domain adaptation settings. Comparisons were made against state-of-the-art adversarial-based, divergence-based, optimal transport-based, and multi-source/source-private specific algorithms.",
    "Limitations": "The paper highlights that while domain adaptation helps, it cannot entirely eliminate dataset bias due to the 'combinatorial nature of too many exogenous factors'. The framework is designed for closed-category domain adaptation, assuming source and target domains share the same label space. Performance, while competitive, showed a slight drop in the source-private setting compared to scenarios with direct source data access. The effectiveness of the method relies on specific design choices, such as the probabilistic bi-directional transport framework, the gradient-stopping strategy for prototypes, and the use of cosine dissimilarity as the cost function, implying that alternative choices might lead to suboptimal results. The EM algorithm for estimating target class proportions requires careful tuning of parameters like β0, and its performance can be sensitive to this choice.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper does not explicitly detail future research directions. However, logical extensions could include exploring different probabilistic transport frameworks or alternative point-to-point cost functions for feature alignment. Further research could also focus on developing methods to address the fundamental challenge of domain shift caused by a combinatorial explosion of exogenous factors, potentially extending beyond closed-set domain adaptation to tackle open-set or universal domain adaptation scenarios. Additionally, investigating the application of this prototype-oriented framework to other domain adaptation tasks (e.g., semantic segmentation, object detection) or refining the EM algorithm for class proportion estimation to be even more robust under extreme class imbalances could be valuable.",
    "Experiment Code": "import torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\n\nclass ProtoLoss(nn.Module):\n    \"\"\"\n    Parameters:\n        - **nav_t** (float): temperature parameter (1 for all experiments)\n        - **beta** (float): learning rate/momentum update parameter for learning target proportions\n        - **num_classes** (int): total number of classes\n        - **s_par** (float, optional): coefficient in front of the bi-directional loss. 0.5 corresponds to pct. 1 corresponds to using only t to mu. 0 corresponds to only using mu to t.\n\n    Inputs: mu_s, f_t\n        - **mu_s** (tensor): weight matrix of the linear classifier, :math:`mu^s`\n        - **f_t** (tensor): feature representations on target domain, :math:`f^t`\n\n    Shape:\n        - mu_s: : math: `(K,F)`, f_t: :math:`(M, F)` where F means the dimension of input features.\n\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, nav_t: float, beta: float, num_classes: int, device: torch.device, s_par: float = 0.5, reduction: str = 'mean'):\n        super(ProtoLoss, self).__init__()\n        self.nav_t = nav_t\n        self.s_par = s_par\n        self.beta = beta\n        self.prop = (torch.ones((num_classes,1))*(1/num_classes)).to(device)\n        self.eps = 1e-6\n         \n    def pairwise_cosine_dist(self, x, y):\n        x = F.normalize(x, p=2, dim=1)\n        y = F.normalize(y, p=2, dim=1)\n        return 1 - torch.matmul(x, y.T)\n\n    def get_pos_logits(self, sim_mat, prop):\n        log_prior = torch.log(prop + self.eps)\n        return sim_mat/self.nav_t + log_prior\n\n    def update_prop(self, prop):\n        return (1 - self.beta) * self.prop + self.beta * prop \n\n    def forward(self, mu_s: torch.Tensor, f_t: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:\n        # Update proportions\n        sim_mat = torch.matmul(mu_s, f_t.T)\n        old_logits = self.get_pos_logits(sim_mat.detach(), self.prop)\n        s_dist_old = F.softmax(old_logits, dim=0)\n        prop = s_dist_old.mean(1, keepdim=True)\n        self.prop = self.update_prop(prop)\n\n        # Calculate bi-directional transport loss\n        new_logits = self.get_pos_logits(sim_mat, self.prop)\n        s_dist = F.softmax(new_logits, dim=0)\n        t_dist = F.softmax(sim_mat/self.nav_t, dim=1)\n        cost_mat = self.pairwise_cosine_dist(mu_s, f_t)\n        source_loss = (self.s_par*cost_mat*s_dist).sum(0).mean() \n        target_loss = (((1-self.s_par)*cost_mat*t_dist).sum(1)*self.prop.squeeze(1)).sum()\n        loss = source_loss + target_loss\n        return loss\n\n\nclass ImageClassifier(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, backbone: nn.Module, num_classes: int, bottleneck: Optional[nn.Module] = None,\n                 bottleneck_dim: Optional[int] = -1, head: Optional[nn.Module] = None):\n        super(ImageClassifier, self).__init__()\n        self.backbone = backbone\n        self.num_classes = num_classes\n        if bottleneck is None:\n            self.bottleneck = nn.Identity()\n            self._features_dim = backbone.out_features\n        else:\n            self.bottleneck = bottleneck\n            assert bottleneck_dim > 0\n            self._features_dim = bottleneck_dim\n\n        if head is None:\n            self.head = nn.Linear(self._features_dim, num_classes)\n        else:\n            self.head = head\n\n    @property\n    def features_dim(self) -> int:\n        return self._features_dim\n\n    def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> Tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:\n        f = self.backbone(x)\n        f = f.view(-1, self.backbone.out_features)\n        f = self.bottleneck(f)\n        predictions = self.head(f)\n        return predictions, f\n\n    def get_parameters(self) -> List[Dict]:\n        params = [\n            {\"params\": self.backbone.parameters(), \"lr_mult\": 0.1},\n            {\"params\": self.bottleneck.parameters(), \"lr_mult\": 1.},\n            {\"params\": self.head.parameters(), \"lr_mult\": 1.},\n        ]\n        return params\n",
    "Experiment Result": "The Prototype-Oriented Conditional Transport (PCT) method is implemented using a custom `ProtoLoss` module for the bi-directional transport and iterative proportion estimation, combined with a standard `ImageClassifier` for feature extraction and prototype learning. \n\n**Key Components:**\n- **Prototypes**: The weights of the final linear layer (`classifier.head.weight`) are interpreted as learnable prototype parameters in the latent space.\n- **Cross-entropy loss**: `F.cross_entropy(y_s, labels_s)` is used for supervised classification on source data.\n- **Bi-directional Transport Loss (`ProtoLoss`)**: Calculates `source_loss` (target features to prototypes, `Lt→µ`) and `target_loss` (prototypes to target features, `Lµ→t`) using cosine dissimilarity. The total transfer loss is their sum.\n- **Target Class Proportion Estimation**: Implemented within `ProtoLoss` using an iterative update (`update_prop`) based on a softened assignment (`s_dist_old.mean(1, keepdim=True)`), akin to an EM algorithm. The proportions (`self.prop`) are initialized uniformly.\n\n**Experimental Setup (from `examples/proto.py`):**\n- **Datasets**: Configurable via `--data`, `--source`, `--target` arguments. Examples include Office31, OfficeHome, DomainNet.\n- **Architecture**: Configurable via `--arch`, e.g., 'resnet18' (pretrained on ImageNet).\n- **Input Preprocessing**: Images are resized to 256, then a random crop of 224x224, random horizontal flip, and normalized using ImageNet means and standard deviations for training. For validation/testing, they are resized to 256, center-cropped to 224, and normalized.\n- **Optimization**: SGD optimizer with momentum and weight decay.\n  - **Learning Rate**: Initial learning rate (`--lr`, default: 0.01) decayed stepwise (`StepwiseLR`) with `gamma` (default: 0.0002) and `decay_rate` (0.75).\n  - **Momentum**: `--momentum` (default: 0.9).\n  - **Weight Decay**: `--wd` (default: 1e-3).\n  - **Layer-wise Learning Rates**: Backbone parameters have a learning rate multiplier of 0.1, while bottleneck and head layers have a multiplier of 1.0.\n- **Training Epochs**: `--epochs` (default: 20).\n- **Iterations per Epoch**: `--iters-per-epoch` (default: 1000).\n- **Batch Sizes**: `--batch-size` for source (default: 32), `--bs_tgt` for target (default: 96).\n- **Loss Hyper-parameters**:\n  - **Trade-off for Transfer Loss**: `--trade-off` (default: 1.0) weights the `ProtoLoss` component.\n  - **Temperature for Navigator**: `--nav_t` (default: 1.0) used in `ProtoLoss` for softmax scaling.\n  - **Momentum for Proportion Update**: `--beta` (default: 0) for updating target class proportions, also decayed stepwise using `beta_scheduler`.\n  - **Coefficient for Bi-directional Loss**: `--s_par` (default: 0.5) balances `source_loss` and `target_loss` in `ProtoLoss`.\n- **Random Seed**: `--seed` (default: None), if set, ensures reproducibility.\n- **Data Loading Workers**: `--workers` (default: 2).\n- **Subsampling**: `--sub_s` and `--sub_t` flags to enable subsampling for source and target datasets, respectively (default: False).\n- **Evaluation**: Accuracy is reported after each epoch on a validation set and finally on a test set. The validation uses `ProtoLoss.get_pos_logits` output for prediction."
}{
    "Title": "Online Prototype Alignment for Few-shot Policy Transfer",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Online Prototype Alignment (OPA), a novel framework for few-shot policy transfer in Reinforcement Learning, specifically addressing domain adaptation challenges where source and target domains are visually distinct and traditional methods relying on visual clues often fail or require abundant target domain data. OPA introduces an exploration mechanism that interacts with unseen elements in the target domain to determine functional similarities, rather than visual appearances, enabling efficient mapping and policy transfer within a few episodes. Experimental results demonstrate OPA's superior transfer performance with significantly fewer target domain samples compared to prior methods, especially in visually divergent environments.",
    "Methodology": "OPA assumes an object-oriented state space where elements share functionalities within prototypes. It consists of three main stages: 1. Novelty Detection: An auto-encoder trained on source domain objects identifies unseen objects in the target domain based on reconstruction loss, mapping them to a special 'unseen prototype' space. 2. Online Prototype Alignment: An exploration policy (πexp) and an inference model (qθ) are trained in simulated unseen environments derived from the source domain. πexp maximizes the mutual information between its trajectories and the original prototypes of unseen elements, guided by an intrinsic reward from qθ that indicates how informative a transition is for prototype inference. This allows efficient learning of exploration behaviors without direct access to the target domain or its ground truth prototypes. 3. Policy Reuse: In the target domain, πexp is run for several episodes to gather information. qθ infers prototype distributions, which are then used to train a lightweight classifier (fcls) that maps unseen objects to their inferred prototypes. This combined mapping function (fproto|T) allows the task policy (πtask), originally trained in the source domain, to be directly applied to the target domain.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on two environments: the object-centric task suite Hunter (Yi et al., 2022) and the more complex 2D Minecraft-like environment Crafter (Hafner, 2022) (detailed in Appendix). For Hunter, the original version served as the source domain, and a new variant with significantly altered object appearances but preserved functionalities was used as the target. Hunter-Z1C1 to Hunter-Z4C4 variants were used. Object representations were derived by dividing 64x64x3 images into 8x8 tiles. For Crafter, object textures were replaced in the target domain. OPA was compared against DARLA, LUSR, UNIT4RL, LTMBR, and a standard PPO baseline. OPA's πtask was trained for 25M steps, πexp for 10M steps (both in source), and required approximately 100 steps (4 episodes) in the target domain for prototype inference. Baselines either trained longer (e.g., DARLA/LUSR for 100M steps) or required millions of fine-tuning steps in the target domain (UNIT4RL/LTMBR). Performance was measured by episode returns and the ratio of target to source domain performance. Ablation studies investigated the quality of prototype alignment and the necessity of πexp compared to random or task policies.",
    "Limitations": "The paper acknowledges that OPA introduces several stages (novelty detection, inference model, exploration policy, classifier) that *may* lead to cumulative errors. However, it argues that errors from novelty detection and the final classifier are minimal in many cases due to the relatively simple and small object space (e.g., 4 objects in Hunter, 19 in Crafter). The quality of the exploration policy's training is dependent on the inference model, an unavoidable interdependency. The framework assumes that source and target domains share the same underlying dynamics and reward structures. Furthermore, the core assumption that elements can be divided into prototypes sharing the same functionalities is fundamental to the approach. Prior methods often suffer from requiring abundant target domain data or failing when visual clues are insufficient for domain adaptation, issues OPA aims to overcome.",

self.obj_id[pos]\n    if obj in self.object_map:\n      self.remove(pos, check_hard=False)\n      self.object_map[obj].append(pos2) #= list(set(self.object_map[obj]) - set([pos]))\n\n    \n  def step(self, a):\n    a = int(a)\n    ag_pos = self.object_map['agent']\n    assert len(ag_pos) == 1\n    ag_pos = ag_pos[0]\n    a_type = self.act_map[a]\n    reward = 0\n    done = False\n    if a_type in ['U', 'D', 'L', 'R']:\n      try_pos = ag_pos[0] - int(a_type == 'U') + int(a_type == 'D'), ag_pos[1] - int(a_type=='L') + int(a_type=='R') \n      if self.check_bound(try_pos):\n        obj_type = self.idx2obj_map[self.state[try_pos]]\n        if obj_type  == 'none':\n          self.move(ag_pos, try_pos)\n        elif obj_type == 'zombie':\n          reward -= 1\n          self.remove(try_pos)\n          self.move(ag_pos, try_pos)\n          # done = True\n        elif obj_type == 'cow':\n          self.remove(try_pos)\n          self.move(ag_pos, try_pos)\n          reward += 1\n    elif 'F' in a_type:\n      ax, ay = ag_pos\n      rew_map = {'zombie':1, 'cow':-1}\n      # rew_map = {'zombie':1, 'cow':0}\n      for k, r in rew_map.items():\n        for ex, ey in self.object_map[k]:\n          assert (ex, ey) != (ax, ay)\n          destroyed = False\n          if ex == ax:\n            destroyed = (ey > ay and a_type == 'FR') or (ey < ay  and a_type == 'FL')\n          if ey == ay:\n            destroyed = (ex > ax and a_type == 'FD') or (ex < ax  and a_type == 'FU')\n          if destroyed and abs(ex - ax) <= self.fire_range and abs(ey - ay) <= self.fire_range:\n            self.remove((ex, ey))\n            reward += r\n    # randomly move enemies\n    if self.zombie_movable and self.cur_len % 2 == 1:\n      for obj_type in      a_type = np.random.choice(['U', 'D', 'L', 'R'])\n          try_pos = ex - int(a_type == 'U') + int(a_type == 'D'), ey - int(a_type=='L') + int(a_type=='R') \n          if self.check_bound(try_pos):\n            obj_type2 = self.idx2obj_map[self.state[try_pos]]\n            if obj_type2 == 'agent':\n              # reward -= 1\n              reward += 1 if obj_type2 == 'cow' else -1\n              # done = True\n              self.remove((ex, ey))\n            elif obj_type2 == 'none':\n              self.move((ex, ey), try_pos)\n    # Delayed Done.\n    # done = done or self.next_done\n    cur_done = done\n    done = self.next_done\n    self.next_done = cur_done or self.next_done\n    clear = ((len(self.object_map['cow']) + len(self.object_map['zombie'])) == 0)\n    if clear:\n      reward += self.clear_reward\n    self.cur_len += 1\n    if self.cur_len >= self.max_len or clear:\n      self.next_done = True\n    if self.fix_len:\n      done = self.cur_len >= self.max_len\n    ag_pos = self.object_map['agent']\n    assert len(ag_pos) == 1\n    ag_pos = ag_pos[0]\n    if self.abs_rew:\n      reward = abs(reward)\n    return self.render(), reward, done, self.obj_id.copy(),\n              num_cow=len(self.object_map['cow']))\n\nimport copy\n\nclass HunterObjEnv(gym.Env):\n  def __init__(self, cfg, version, env_version=None, ret_img=False):\n trajs.obs.label.reshape(B,T,-1)\n        label = (label * mask.reshape(B,T,1)).long()\n\n        loss = self.loss_fn(logits.reshape(B*T*C, -1), label.flatten())\n        loss = loss + addi_loss\n        targ_logp = torch.distributions.Categorical(logits=logits).log_prob(label) * (label > 0)\n        max_targ_p = torch.where(label > 0.5, targ_logp, torch.ones_like(targ_logp) * -100.0).amax(1)[label.amax(1) > 0.5].exp().mean().item()\n        mean_targ_p = targ_logp[label>0.5].exp().mean().item()\n        prob_refs = torch.eye(C) * (1-self.ref_gru.mask) + probs * self.ref_gru.mask\n        other_info = {'targ_p': targ_logp.exp() * (label > 0), \n                    'hidden_state': hidden_state,\n                    'prob_refs': prob_refs,\n                    'targ_logp': targ_logp * (label > 0.5) + (label < 0.5) * -1000.0, \n                    'probs': probs, 'logits': logits,\n                    'max_targ_p': max_targ_p, 'mean_targ_p': mean_targ_p,\n                    'embeds': embeds,\n                    }\n        return targ_logp, loss, hidden_state, other_info\n    def label_rewards(self, trajs, masks, hidden_state=None):\n        B, T, C = trajs.obs.label.shape\n        label = (trajs.obs.label * masks.reshape(B,T,1)).long()\n        if hidden_state is not None:\n            rst_hid = False\n            refs, _ = torch.distributions.Categorical(logits=logits).log_prob(label[:,0])\n        initial_targ_logp = (initial_targ_logp * (label[:,0] > 0.5)).sum(-1)\n        targ_logp, loss, hidden_state, other_infos = self._compute_context(trajs, masks, hidden_state)\n        targ_logp = targ_logp.sum(-1)\n        rews = targ_logp - torch.cat([initial_targ_logp[:,None], targ_logp[:,:-1]], dim=1)\n        if self.cfg.get('clip_rews', False):\n            rews = torch.clamp(rews, min=0) * (targ_logp.exp() >= 0.5)\n        if rst_hid:\n            loss = loss + addi_loss\n        return to_numpy(rews) * net_arch[-1] if len(net_arch) > 0 else input_dim\n        modules.append(create_layer(last_layer_dim, output_dim))\n    if squash_output:\n        modules.append(nn.Tanh())\n    if return_seq:\n      modules = nn.Sequential(*modules)\n      apply_init(modules)\n    return modules\n\n\nclass MultiLinear(nn.Module):\n  def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, num_linears=2, add_bias=True,):\n    super().__init__()\n    self.W = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(num_linears, in_channels, out_channels))\n    self.b = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(num_linears, out_channels))\n    self.add_bias = add_bias\n    self.in_channels = in_channels\n    self.out_channels = out_channels\n    self.num_linears = num_linears\n    nn.init.orthogonal_(self.W, nn.init.calculate_gain('relu'))\n  @staticmethod\n  def make_layer(num_linears):\n    def create_layer(din, dout):\n      return MultiLinear(din, dout, num_linears)\n    return create_layer\n  @staticmethod\n  def broadcast(x, num_linears):\n    shape = list(x.shape)\n    extended_shape = shape[:-1] + [num_linears] + shape[-1:] \n    return x.unsqueeze(-2).expand(extended_shape)\n  @staticmethod\n  def reduce(x, reduce_type):\n    if reduce_type == 'sum':\n      return x.sum(-2)\n    elif reduce_type == 'max':\n      return x.amax(-2) \n    elif reduce_type == 'mean':\n      return x.mean(-2)\n    elif reduce_type == 'flat':\n      return x.reshape(np.prod(x.shape[:-1]), x.shape[-1])\n  \"\"\"\n    input:  (..., n, cin)\n    output: (..., n, cout)\n  \"\"\"\n  def forward(self, x):\n    x = x.unsqueeze(-2)               # (..., n, 1, in_channels)\n    out = torch.matmul(x, self.W)     # (..., n, 1, out_channels)\n    out = out.squeeze(-2)\n    if self.add_bias:\n      out = out + self.b    # (..., n, out_channels)\n    return MultiAttention(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, dim, heads, dim_head=None, dropout = 0.,\n                qdim=None, kdim=None, vdim=None,\n                layer_norm=False,\n                to_q_net=[], to_k_net=[], to_v_net=[], to_out_net=[]):\n      super().__init__()\n      if dim_head is None:\n        dim_head = dim // heads\n      self.dim = dim\n      self.heads = heads\n      self.dim_head = dim_head\n      qdim = dim if qdim is None else qdim\n      vdim = dim if vdim is None else vdim\n      kdim = dim if kdim is None else kdim\n      self.scale = dim_head ** -0.5\n      self.to_q = create_mlp(qdim, heads * dim_head, to_q_net, return_seq=True)\n      self.to_k = create_mlp(kdim, heads * dim_head, to_k_net, return_seq=True)\n      self.to_v = create_mlp(vdim, heads * dim_head, to_v_net, return_seq=True)\n      self.to_out = create_mlp(dim_head*heads, dim, to_out_net, return_seq=True)\n      self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(qdim) if layer_norm else nn.Identity()\n      self.ac = nn.Softmax(-1)\n    def forward(self, q, k, v, mask=None, attn_dim=None, q_fn=None, hard=False, tau=1.):\n      \"\"\"\n        input: q(L, B, qE), kv(N, B, E)\n        output: out(L, B, E), atten(B L N)\n        mask: (B, h, L, N)\n      \"\"\"\n      origin_q = q\n      qkv = self.to_q(q), self.to_k(k), self.to_v(v)\n      q, k, v = map(lambda t: rearrange(t, 'n b (h d) -> b h n d', h = self.heads), qkv)\n      dots = einsum('b h i d, b h j d -> b h i j', q, k) * self.scale\n      if not hard:\n        attn = self.ac(dots)  # (b, h, l, n)\n        if q_fn is not None: \n          attn = q_fn(attn) # b h l n\n        if mask is not None:\n          assert torch.all(mask >= 0)\n          attn = attn * mask\n          attn = attn / (attn.sum(-1, keepdim=True) + 1e-4)\n      else:\n        if mask is not None:\n          dots = dots * mask + (1 - mask) * -1e4\n        # L.record_mean('atten_ent', torch.distributions.Categorical(logits=dots).entropy().mean())\n        attn = F.gumbel_softmax(dots, tau=tau, hard=True)\n      out = einsum('b h i j, b h j d -> b h i d', attn, v)\n      out = rearrange(out, 'b h n d -> n b (h d)')\n      out =  self.to_out(out)\n      out = dots\n      else:\n        return out, attn[:,attn_dim,...]\n\nclass QueryMultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):\n  def __init__(self, L, E, head_num = 4, qE=None, kE=None, vE=None, **kwargs):\n nn.Conv2d(in_channels=channels,\n                               out_channels=channels,\n                               kernel_size=3,\n                               padding=1)\n    def forward(self, ConvSequence(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, input_shape, out_channels):\n        super(ConvSequence, self).__init__()\n        self._input_shape = input_shape\n        self._out_channels =                   padding=1)\n        self.max_pool2d = nn.MaxPool2d(kernel_size=3,\n                                       stride=2,\n                                       padding=1)\n        self.res_block0 = ResidualBlock(self._out_channels)\n        self.res_block1 = ResidualBlock(self._out_channels)\n    def forward(self, x):\n        x = self.conv(x)\n        x = self.max_pool2d(x)\n        x = self.res_block0(x)\n        x = self.res_block1(x)\n        return x\n    def get_output_shape(self):\n        _c, h, w = self._input_shape\n        return self._out_channels, (h + 1) // 2, conv_seq.get_output_shape()\n        conv_seqs.append(conv_seq)\n    return conv_seqs, shape\n\nclass NormAndTrans(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, norm=True, inds=[0,3,1,2], device=None):\n device=self.device, dtype=torch.float32)\n        if self.norm:\n            x = x / 255. \n        assert len(x.shape) == len(self.inds), f'NormAndTrans: {x.shape} v.s. {self.inds}'\n        return x.permute(*self.inds)\n\nclass ImpalaEncoder(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, obs_space, latent_dim=256, activation_fn=nn.ReLU, lnorm=False, flatten=True, channels=[16,32,32]):\n }{
    "Title": "Mind the Gap Between Prototypes and Images in Cross-domain Finetuning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the implicit assumption in cross-domain few-shot classification (CFC) that prototype and image instance embeddings share the same representation transformation. It validates that a natural \"modality gap\" exists between these embeddings and demonstrates that shared transformations shrink this gap, hindering optimal representation learning and leading to poorly clustered representations. The main contribution is the proposal of Contrastive Prototype-Image Adaptation (CoPA), a simple yet effective method that adapts different transformations for prototypes and image instances, akin to CLIP, by treating prototypes as text prompts. CoPA achieves state-of-the-art performance on Meta-Dataset, learns better representation clusters by enlarging the prototype-image gap, and reaches minimal validation loss at this enlarged gap.",
    "Methodology": "CoPA is built upon a frozen pre-trained ResNet-18 backbone. It adapts two distinct transformation heads: `hθP` for prototypes and `hθI` for image instances, in contrast to prior methods like URL that use a shared transformation. The core learning objective is a symmetric cross-entropy (SCE) loss, commonly used in contrastive learning. Prototypes are generated by averaging instance embeddings within a class, and these are treated conceptually like text prompts in CLIP, depicting higher-level, class-specific information. The two different transformations preserve discriminative information in gradients and expand the hypothesis space, while the contrastive objective facilitates maintaining and enlarging the modality gap between prototype and image representations, which is shown to improve generalization.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on the Meta-Dataset benchmark. Two main settings were used: \"train on all datasets\" (8 seen domains including ImageNet, Omniglot, Aircraft, etc., and 5 unseen domains like Traffic Sign, MSCOCO, MNIST, CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100) and \"train on ImageNet only\" (ImageNet as the sole seen domain). Tasks were typically sampled in a vary-way vary-shot manner, with additional evaluations on 5-way 1-shot and vary-way 5-shot tasks. A frozen pre-trained ResNet-18 backbone was used. Adaptation modules were primarily linear transformation heads, with additional experiments using Visual Transformer (ViT) heads. The Adam optimizer was used for adaptation, with specific learning rates and weight decays per dataset. All results, including comparisons with baselines like URL, TSA, and TA2-Net, were averaged over 5 runs with different random seeds (41-45) to report mean accuracy and 95% confidence intervals. Experiments were run on an NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3090 GPU.",
    "Limitations": "One potential limitation of the proposed CoPA method is its reliance on symmetric cross-entropy loss, which is common in contrastive learning. This loss tends to perform better with larger batch sizes. The paper notes that while CoPA still achieves relatively good performance, its effectiveness tends to degrade when the size of the data batch becomes small, thereby constraining its application in scenarios with extremely few data samples. For example, performance on 5-way 1-shot tasks, where prototypes are generated from a single sample, showed that overfitting can occur and CoPA's advantages diminish.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The research suggests a future direction where abstract concept information can be extracted from available single-modal data (like prototypes) and then used to train or adapt models via methods like contrastive learning. This approach aims to learn more discriminative and useful representations for downstream tasks. Given the diversity of visual information compared to text, this line of research is identified as a promising and feasible way to develop more general models for a wide range of visual tasks, potentially contributing to more robust and adaptable vision systems.",
    "Experiment Code": "import torch.nn.functional as F\n\ndef compute_prototypes(embeddings:torch.tensor, labels:torch.tensor) -> torch.tensor:\n    unique_labels = torch.range(start=0, end=torch.max(labels)).unsqueeze(dim=1).type_as(labels)\n    indicate_mat = unique_labels.eq(labels.reshape(1, list(labels.shape)[0])).type_as(embeddings)\n    flatten_protos = torch.matmul(indicate_mat, embeddings.flatten(1)) / indicate_mat.sum(dim=1, keepdim=True)\n    _, c, h, w = embeddings.size()\n    prototypes = flatten_protos.view(flatten_protos.size(0), c, h, w)\n    return prototypes\n\ndef pa(features, vartheta):\n    features = F.conv2d(features, vartheta[0]).flatten(1)\n    return features\n\ndef pa_adaptation(context_feat:torch.tensor, proto_feat:torch.tensor, context_labels:torch.tensor,\n                  target_feat:torch.tensor, target_labels:torch.tensor,\n                  prototype_encoder, feature_encoder, dataset_name:str, max_iter:int=50) -> dict:\n    data_recorder = {'train_losses': [], 'train_accs': [], 'val_losses': [], 'val_accs': []}\n    if args['encoder.type'] == 'transformer':\n        prototype_encoder.reset_params()\n        prototype_encoder.to(device)\n        feature_encoder.reset_params()\n        feature_encoder.to(device)\n        lr = 1e-3 if dataset_name not in ['traffic_sign', 'mnist'] else 1e-3\n        weight_decay = 0.1 if dataset_name not in ['traffic_sign', 'mnist'] else 0.0\n        optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([{'params': feature_encoder.parameters()},\n                                      {'params': prototype_encoder.parameters()}],\n                                      lr=lr, weight_decay=weight_decay)\n    else:\n        feat_dim = context_feat.size(1)\n        feat_linear_vars = [torch.eye(feat_dim, feat_dim).unsqueeze(-1).unsqueeze(-1).to(device).requires_grad_(True)]\n        proto_linear_vars = [torch.eye(feat_dim, feat_dim).unsqueeze(-1).unsqueeze(-1).to(device).requires_grad_(True)]\n        lr = 1e-3 if dataset_name not in ['traffic_sign', 'mnist'] else 5e-3\n        weight_decay = 0.1 if dataset_name not in ['traffic_sign', 'mnist'] else 0.0\n        optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([{'params': feat_linear_vars},\n                                      {'params': proto_linear_vars,}],\n                                      lr=lr, weight_decay=weight_decay)\n    prototypes = compute_prototypes(proto_feat, context_labels)\n    for i in range(max_iter):\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            if feature_encoder is not None:\n                prototype_encoder.eval()\n                feature_encoder.eval()\n                transformed_target_feats = feature_encoder(target_feat)\n                transformed_prototypes = prototype_encoder(prototypes)\n            else:\n                target_feat = F.adaptive_avg_pool2d(target_feat, (1, 1))\n                prototypes = F.adaptive_avg_pool2d(prototypes, (1, 1))\n                transformed_target_feats = pa(target_feat, feat_linear_vars)\n                transformed_prototypes = pa(prototypes, proto_linear_vars)\n            _, dynamic_dict = symmetric_CE(transformed_target_feats.flatten(1), transformed_prototypes.flatten(1), target_labels)\n            data_recorder['val_losses'].append(dynamic_dict['loss'])\n            data_recorder['val_accs'].append(dynamic_dict['acc'])\n            if feature_encoder is not None:\n                feature_encoder.train()\n                prototype_encoder.train()\n        optimizer.zero_grad()\n        if feature_encoder is not None:\n            transformed_context_feats = feature_encoder(context_feat)\n            transformed_prototypes = prototype_encoder(prototypes)\n        else:\n            context_feat = F.adaptive_avg_pool2d(context_feat, (1, 1))\n            prototypes = F.adaptive_avg_pool2d(prototypes, (1, 1))\n            transformed_context_feats = pa(context_feat, feat_linear_vars)\n            transformed_prototypes = pa(prototypes, proto_linear_vars)\n        loss, train_dynamic = symmetric_CE(transformed_context_feats.flatten(1),\n                                           transformed_prototypes.flatten(1), context_labels)\n        data_recorder['train_losses'].append(train_dynamic['loss'])\n        data_recorder['train_accs'].append(train_dynamic['acc'])\n        loss.backward()\n        optimizer.step()\n        if i == max_iter - 1:\n            with torch.no_grad():\n                if feature_encoder is not None:\n                    prototype_encoder.eval()\n                    feature_encoder.eval()\n                    transformed_target_feats = feature_encoder(target_feat)\n                    transformed_prototypes = prototype_encoder(prototypes)\n                else:\n                    target_feat = F.adaptive_avg_pool2d(target_feat, (1, 1))\n                    prototypes = F.adaptive_avg_pool2d(prototypes, (1, 1))\n                    transformed_target_feats = pa(target_feat, feat_linear_vars)\n                    transformed_prototypes = pa(prototypes, proto_linear_vars)\n                _, dynamic_dict = symmetric_CE(transformed_target_feats.flatten(1), transformed_prototypes.flatten(1), target_labels)\n                data_recorder['val_losses'].append(dynamic_dict['loss'])\n                data_recorder['val_accs'].append(dynamic_dict['acc'])\n    return data_recorder\n\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\n\ndef symmetric_CE(embeddings:torch.tensor, protos:torch.tensor, labels:torch.tensor) -> torch.tensor:\n    embeddings = embeddings / embeddings.norm(dim=1, keepdim=True)\n    protos = protos / protos.norm(dim=1, keepdim=True)\n    one_hot_labels = F.one_hot(labels)\n    proto_labels = one_hot_labels.type_as(protos) @ protos\n    logits = torch.exp(torch.tensor(args['SCE.tau'])) * embeddings @ proto_labels.t().type_as(embeddings)\n    pseudo_labels = torch.arange(logits.size(0), device=device).long()\n    loss = 0.5 * (F.cross_entropy(logits, pseudo_labels) + F.cross_entropy(logits.t(), pseudo_labels))\n    acc_logits = F.cosine_similarity(embeddings.unsqueeze(1),\n                                 protos.unsqueeze(0),\n                                 dim=-1,\n                                 eps=1e-30) * 10\n    log_p_y = F.log_softmax(acc_logits, dim=1)\n    preds = log_p_y.argmax(1)\n    acc = torch.eq(preds, labels.type(torch.long)).float().mean()\n    dynamic_dict = {'loss': loss.item(), 'acc': acc.item()}\n    return loss, dynamic_dict\n\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch\n\nclass AttentionModule(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, num_heads:int, in_channels: int=512, out_channels: int=512,\n                 is_bias: bool=True, init_mode:str='random') -> None:\n        super(AttentionModule, self).__init__()\n        self.num_heads = num_heads\n        self.init_mode = init_mode\n        self.out_dim = out_channels\n        self.q_head = nn.Linear(in_features=in_channels, out_features=out_channels, bias=is_bias)\n        self.k_head = nn.Linear(in_features=in_channels, out_features=out_channels, bias=is_bias)\n        self.v_head = nn.Linear(in_features=in_channels, out_features=out_channels, bias=is_bias)\n        self._init_params()\n    \n    def forward(self, x: torch.tensor) -> torch.tensor:\n        def split_last(X: torch.tensor, shape: tuple) -> torch.tensor:\n            shape = list(shape)\n            assert shape.count(-1) <= 1\n            if -1 in shape:\n                shape[shape.index(-1)] = int(X.size(-1) / -np.prod(shape))\n            return X.view(*X.size()[:-1], *shape)\n        def merge_last(X: torch.tensor, num_dims: int) -> torch.tensor:\n            size = X.size()\n            assert num_dims > 1 and num_dims < len(size)\n            return X.view(*size[:-num_dims], -1)\n        def softmax_func(score_mat: torch.tensor) -> torch.tensor:\n            max_vals, _ = torch.max(score_mat, dim=-1, keepdim=True)\n            score_mat -= max_vals\n            exp_logits = torch.exp(score_mat)\n            softmax_mat = exp_logits / torch.sum(exp_logits, dim=-1, keepdim=True)\n            return softmax_mat\n        queries, keys, values = self.q_head(x), self.k_head(x), self.v_head(x)\n        queries, keys, values = (split_last(x, (self.num_heads, -1)).transpose(1, 2) for x in [queries, keys, values])\n        sim_score = softmax_func(torch.matmul(queries, keys.transpose(-2, -1)) * torch.rsqrt(torch.tensor(keys.size(-1)).type_as(keys)).to(keys.device))\n        atten_feat = torch.matmul(sim_score, values).transpose(1, 2).contiguous()\n        atten_feat = merge_last(atten_feat, 2)\n        return atten_feat\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def _init_params(self) -> None:\n        if self.init_mode == 'random':\n            nn.init.xavier_normal_(self.q_head.weight)\n            if hasattr(self.q_head, 'bias') and self.q_head.bias is not None:\n                nn.init.normal_(self.q_head.bias)\n            nn.init.xavier_normal_(self.k_head.weight)\n            if hasattr(self.k_head, 'bias') and self.k_head.bias is not None:\n                nn.init.normal_(self.k_head.bias)\n            nn.init.xavier_normal_(self.v_head.weight)\n            if hasattr(self.v_head, 'bias') and self.v_head.bias is not None:\n                nn.init.normal_(self.v_head.bias)\n        elif self.init_mode == 'eye':\n            self.q_head.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.eye(self.out_dim, self.out_dim))\n            self.q_head.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros_like(self.q_head.bias))\n            self.k_head.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.eye(self.out_dim, self.out_dim))\n            self.k_head.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros_like(self.k_head.bias))\n            self.v_head.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.eye(self.out_dim, self.out_dim))\n            self.v_head.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros_like(self.v_head.bias))\n        else:\n            raise ValueError(\"Unrecognized initialization mode. Please choose from ['random', 'eye].\")\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def reset_params(self) -> None:\n        self._init_params()\n\nclass SimpleSelfAttentionBlock(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, num_heads:int=8, in_channels:int=512, out_channels:int=512,\n                 is_bias:bool=True, init_mode:str='eye'):\n        super(SimpleSelfAttentionBlock, self).__init__()\n        self.out_dim = out_channels\n        self.init_mode = init_mode\n        self.atten_module = AttentionModule(num_heads=num_heads, in_channels=in_channels, out_channels=out_channels,\n                                            is_bias=is_bias, init_mode=init_mode)\n        self.layernorm = nn.LayerNorm(out_channels, eps=1e-6)\n        self.linear_head = nn.Linear(in_features=out_channels, out_features=out_channels)\n    \n    def forward(self, x:torch.tensor):\n        return self.linear_head(self.layernorm(x+self.atten_module(x)))\n    \n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def _init_params(self) -> None:\n        if self.init_mode == 'eye':\n            self.linear_head.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.eye(self.out_dim, self.out_dim))\n            self.linear_head.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros_like(self.linear_head.bias.data))\n        elif self.init_mode == 'random':\n            nn.init.xavier_normal_(self.linear_head.weight)\n            if hasattr(self.linear_head, 'bias') and self.linear_head.bias is not None:\n                nn.init.normal_(self.linear_head.bias, std=1e-6)\n        else:\n            raise ValueError(\"Unrecognized initialization mode.        ])\n\n    def forward(self, protos: torch.tensor) -> torch.tensor:\n        protos = torch.transpose(torch.flatten(protos, start_dim=2), 1, 2)\n        protos =               padding=1, bias=False)\n        self.bn1 = nn.BatchNorm2d(self.inplanes)\n        self.relu = nn.ReLU(inplace=True)\n        self.maxpool = nn.MaxPool2d(kernel_size=3, stride=2,     "open_problems": "Prototype-based few-shot classifiers measure the Euclidean (or cosine) distance between the query embedding and each class prototype, but they ignore how *reliable* a prototype is. In practice the small support set yields class–dependent scatter: some classes form tight clusters, others are much noisier. Treating both cases equally lets noisy classes dominate the decision boundary and hurts generalisation, especially in 1-shot/low-shot episodes.",
    "methods": "Variance–Normalised Prototypical Networks (VNP)\n1. Compute the usual class prototype µ_c = 1/K Σ_i φ(x_i).\n2. Estimate a scalar within-class variance\n      v_c = 1/K Σ_i ||φ(x_i) – µ_c||²  (+ ε for numerical stability).\n3. Replace the plain squared Euclidean distance by a variance-normalised distance\n      d_c(q) = ||φ(q) – µ_c||² / v_c^β        (β∈[0,1], default 1).\n   Intuition: classes whose support features are scattered (large v_c) are treated less confidently; tight classes contribute more.\n4. Feed the negative distances to the softmax as usual.\nThe only added cost is one scalar per class per episode; the method is fully differentiable and can be dropped into any ProtoNet implementation. β=0 recovers the baseline.",
    "experimental_setup": "Datasets: miniImageNet and CIFAR-FS, 5-way 1-shot and 5-shot.\nBackbone: frozen ResNet-12 trained with cross-entropy on the base classes (same as in “A Closer Look at Prototype Classifier…”).\nEpisodes: 10 000 for testing.\nBaselines: (i) plain Prototype classifier with L2-normalised features, (ii) VNP with β=1.\nMetric: average classification accuracy with 95 % CI.\nNo additional training is needed – the experiment is a few hundred lines and runs in minutes on a single GPU.",
    "experimental_code": "import torch, torch.nn.functional as F\n\ndef variance_normalised_logits(query, support, support_labels, beta=1.0, eps=1e-6):\n    # query       : [Q , D]\n    # support     : [K*N , D]\n    # support_lbl : [K*N]  values 0..N-1\n    N = support_labels.max().item() + 1\n    D = support.size(1)\n    # prototypes\n    protos = torch.zeros(N, D, device=support.device)\n    protos.index_add_(0, support_labels, support)\n    counts = torch.bincount(support_labels, minlength=N).unsqueeze(1).clamp(min=1)\n    protos = protos / counts\n    # per-class scalar variance\n    var = torch.zeros(N, device=support.device)\n    diff = support - protos[support_labels]\n    var.index_add_(0, support_labels, (diff**2).sum(1))\n    var = var / counts.squeeze(1) + eps   # shape [N]\n    # distances Q×N\n    d2 = ((query.unsqueeze(1) - protos)**2).sum(-1) / (var**beta)\n    return -d2  # logits\n\n# inference loop -------------------------------------------------------------\nwith torch.no_grad():\n    feats = backbone(images)           # get embeddings\n    feats = F.normalize(feats)         # L2 normalise as in baseline\n    q_feat, s_feat = feats[Q_mask], feats[S_mask]\n    logits = variance_normalised_logits(q_feat, s_feat, s_labels)\n    preds  = logits.argmax(1)\n",
    "expected_result": "Across both datasets we expect +1 to +2 percentage-point absolute accuracy over the plain prototype baseline.\nTypical numbers (single run):\nminiImageNet 5-way\n  1-shot : 62.3 → 63.8 (+1.5)\n  5-shot : 79.8 → 81.0 (+1.2)\nCIFAR-FS 5-way\n  1-shot : 70.4 → 71.9 (+1.5)\n  5-shot : 86.1 → 87.0 (+0.9)\nGains are larger when class scatter is heterogeneous (typical in cross-domain tests).",
    "expected_conclusion": "A single scalar factor per class – the sample variance of its support embeddings – is enough to make Prototypical Networks variance-aware. The modification is theoretically sound (normalises distances by estimated noise) and computationally negligible, yet consistently improves few-shot accuracy without additional training or hyper-parameters. This suggests that accounting for prototype reliability is a simple and effective way to close part of the generalisation gap in few-shot classification."
